    17→## WORKFLOW ARCHITECTURE
    18→
    19→This uses **step-file architecture** for disciplined execution.
    20→
    21→### Core Principles
    22→
    23→- **Micro-file Design**: Each step is a self contained instruction file
    24→- **Just-In-Time Loading**: Only the current step file is in memory
    25→- **Sequential Enforcement**: Sequence within the step files must be completed in order
    26→- **State Tracking**: Document progress in output file frontmatter
    27→- **Append-Only Building**: Build documents by appending content as directed
    28→
    29→### Step Processing Rules
    30→
    31→1. **READ COMPLETELY**: Always read the entire step file before taking any action
    32→2. **FOLLOW SEQUENCE**: Execute all numbered sections in order
    33→3. **WAIT FOR INPUT**: If a menu is presented, halt and wait for user selection
    34→4. **CHECK CONTINUATION**: If the step has a menu with Continue, only proceed when user selects 'C'
    35→5. **SAVE STATE**: Update frontmatter before loading next step
    36→6. **LOAD NEXT**: When directed, read fully and follow the next step file
    37→
    38→### Critical Rules
    39→
    40→- 🛑 **NEVER** load multiple step files simultaneously
    41→- 📖 **ALWAYS** read entire step file before execution
    42→- 🚫 **NEVER** skip steps or optimize the sequence

     2→name: 'step-03-extract'
     3→description: 'Tier-dependent source code extraction — AST or source reading for exports, signatures, and types'
     4→nextStepFile: './step-03b-fetch-temporal.md'

     6→---
     7→
     8→# Step 3: Extract
     9→
    10→## STEP GOAL:
    11→
    12→To extract all public exports, function signatures, type definitions, and co-import patterns from the source code using tier-appropriate tools, building a complete extraction inventory with confidence-tiered provenance citations.
    13→
    14→## MANDATORY EXECUTION RULES (READ FIRST):
    15→
    16→### Universal Rules:
    17→
    18→- 📖 CRITICAL: Read the complete step file before taking any action
    19→- 🎯 ALWAYS follow the exact instructions in the step file
    20→- ⚙️ TOOL/SUBPROCESS FALLBACK: If any instruction references a tool you do not have access to, you MUST still achieve the outcome in your main context thread
    21→- ✅ YOU MUST ALWAYS SPEAK OUTPUT in your Agent communication style with the config `{communication_language}`
    22→
    23→### Role Reinforcement:
    24→
    25→- ✅ You are a skill compilation engine performing structural extraction
    26→- ✅ Zero hallucination tolerance — every extracted item must trace to source code
    27→- ✅ Confidence tier labeling is mandatory for every extraction result
    28→
    29→### Step-Specific Rules:
    30→
    31→- 🎯 Focus ONLY on extracting exports, signatures, types from source code
    32→- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to compile SKILL.md content — that's step-05
    33→- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to write any output files — extraction stays in context
    34→- 🚫 FORBIDDEN to include content that cannot be cited to a source location
    35→- ⚒️ Every extracted item MUST have a provenance citation: `[AST:{file}:L{line}]` or `[SRC:{file}:L{line}]`
    36→
    37→## EXECUTION PROTOCOLS:
    38→
    39→- 🎯 Follow MANDATORY SEQUENCE exactly
    40→- 💾 Build extraction inventory in context — do not write files
    41→- 📖 Load extraction patterns data file for tier-specific strategy
    42→- 🚫 If an export cannot be verified, exclude it — do not guess
    43→
    44→## CONTEXT BOUNDARIES:
    45→
    46→- Available: brief_data, tier, source_location, file_tree from step-01; ecosystem_status from step-02
    47→- Focus: Source code extraction and inventory building
    48→- Limits: Do NOT compile, assemble, or write any output
    49→- Dependencies: Source code must be accessible (resolved in step-01)
    50→
    51→## MANDATORY SEQUENCE
    52→
    53→**CRITICAL:** Follow this sequence exactly. Do not skip, reorder, or improvise.
    54→
    55→### 1. Load Extraction Patterns
    56→
    57→Load `{extractionPatternsData}` completely. Identify the strategy for the current forge tier.
    58→
    59→### 2. Apply Scope Filters
    60→
    61→From the brief, apply scope and pattern filters:
    62→
    63→- `scope` — determines what to extract (e.g., "all public exports", specific modules)
    64→- `include_patterns` — file globs to include (if specified)
    65→- `exclude_patterns` — file globs to exclude (if specified)
    66→
    67→Build the filtered file list from the source tree resolved in step-01.
    68→
    69→### 3. Check for Docs-Only Mode
    70→
    71→**If `source_type: "docs-only"` in the brief data:**
    72→
    73→"**Docs-only mode:** No source code to extract. Documentation content will be fetched from `doc_urls` in step-03c."
    74→
    75→Build an empty extraction inventory with zero exports. Set `extraction_mode: "docs-only"` in context. Auto-proceed through Gate 2 (section 5) — display the empty inventory and note that T3 content will be produced by the doc-fetcher step.
    76→
    77→**If `source_type: "source"` (default):** Continue with extraction below.
    78→
    79→### 4. Execute Tier-Dependent Extraction
    80→
    81→**Remote Source Resolution (Forge/Deep only):**
    82→
    83→If `source_repo` is a local path: proceed with the tier-appropriate strategy as normal.
    84→
    85→If `source_repo` is a remote URL (GitHub URL or owner/repo format) AND tier is Forge or Deep:
    86→
    87→1. **Check `git` availability:** Verify `git` is functional (`git --version`). If `git` is not available, skip to the fallback warning below.
    88→
    89→2. **Ephemeral shallow clone:** Clone the repository to a system temp path for AST access:
    90→
    91→   ```
    92→   temp_path = {system_temp}/skf-ephemeral-{skill-name}-{timestamp}/
    93→   git clone --depth 1 --branch {branch} --single-branch --filter=blob:none {source_repo} {temp_path}
    94→   ```
    95→
    96→   If `include_patterns` are specified in the brief, use sparse-checkout to limit the clone scope:
    97→
    98→   ```
    99→   git clone --depth 1 --branch {branch} --single-branch --filter=blob:none --sparse {source_repo} {temp_path}
   100→   git -C {temp_path} sparse-checkout set {include_patterns}
   101→   ```
   102→
   103→3. **If clone succeeds:** Update the working source path to `{temp_path}` for all subsequent AST operations in this step. Proceed with the **Forge/Deep Tier** extraction strategy below. Mark `ephemeral_clone_active = true` for cleanup.
   104→
   105→4. **If clone fails (network error, auth failure, timeout):**
   106→
   107→   ⚠️ **Warn the user explicitly:**
   108→
   109→   "Ephemeral clone of `{source_repo}` failed: {error}. Degrading to source reading (T1-low) for this run. For T1 (AST-verified) confidence, clone the repository locally and update `source_repo` in your brief to the local path."
   110→
   111→   Proceed with Quick tier extraction strategy below. Note the degradation reason in context for the evidence report.
   112→
   113→**Ephemeral clone cleanup:** After extraction is complete for all files in scope (whether successful or partially failed), before presenting the Gate 2 summary (Section 5), if `ephemeral_clone_active`, delete the `{temp_path}` directory. Log: "Ephemeral source clone cleaned up." This ensures cleanup runs even if some extractions failed, as long as the step itself is still executing.
   114→
   115→**Quick Tier (No AST tools):**
   116→
   117→1. Use `gh_bridge.list_tree(owner, repo, branch)` to map source structure (if remote)
   118→2. Identify entry points: index files, main exports, public modules
   119→3. Use `gh_bridge.read_file(owner, repo, path)` to read each entry point
   120→4. Extract from source text: exported function names, parameter lists, return types
   121→5. Infer types from JSDoc, docstrings, type annotations
   122→6. Confidence: All results T1-low — `[SRC:{file}:L{line}]`
   123→
   124→**Forge/Deep Tier (AST available):**
   125→
   126→1. Detect language from brief or file extensions
   127→2. Use `ast_bridge.scan_definitions(path, language)` for each file in scope
   128→3. For each export: extract function name, full signature, parameter types, return type, line number
   129→4. Use `ast_bridge.detect_co_imports(path, libraries[])` to find integration points
   130→5. Build extraction rules YAML data for reproducibility
   131→6. Confidence: All results T1 — `[AST:{file}:L{line}]`
   132→
   133→**If AST tool is unavailable at Forge/Deep tier:**
   134→
   135→⚠️ **Warn the user explicitly:** "AST tools are unavailable — extraction will use source reading (T1-low). Run [SF] Setup Forge to detect and configure AST tools for T1 confidence."
   136→
   137→Degrade to Quick tier extraction. Note the degradation reason in context for the evidence report.
   138→
   139→**For each file — handle failures gracefully:**
   140→
   141→- If a file cannot be read: log warning, skip file, continue with remaining files
   142→- If AST parsing fails on a file: fall back to source reading for that file, continue
   143→
   144→### 4. Build Extraction Inventory
   145→
   146→Compile all extracted data into a structured inventory:
   147→
   148→**Per-export entry:**
   149→- Function/type name
   150→- Full signature with types
   151→- Parameters (name, type, required/optional)
   152→- Return type
   153→- Source file and line number
   154→- Provenance citation (`[AST:...]` or `[SRC:...]`)
   155→- Confidence tier (T1 or T1-low)
   156→
   157→**Aggregate counts:**
   158→- Total files scanned
   159→- Total exports found
   160→- Exports by type (functions, types/interfaces, constants)
   161→- Confidence breakdown (T1 count, T1-low count)
   162→- `top_exports[]` — sorted list of the top 10-20 public API function names by prominence (import frequency or documentation position). This named field is consumed by step-03b for targeted temporal fetching and cache fingerprinting.
   163→
   164→**Co-import patterns (Forge/Deep only):**
   165→- Libraries commonly imported alongside extracted exports
   166→- Integration point suggestions
   167→
   168→### 5. Present Extraction Summary (Gate 2)
   169→
   170→Display the extraction findings for user confirmation:
   171→
   172→"**Extraction complete.**
   173→
   174→**Files scanned:** {file_count}
   175→**Exports found:** {export_count} ({function_count} functions, {type_count} types, {constant_count} constants)
   176→**Confidence:** {t1_count} T1 (AST-verified), {t1_low_count} T1-low (source reading)
   177→**Tier used:** {tier}
   178→**Co-import patterns:** {pattern_count} detected
   179→
   180→**Top exports:**
   181→{list top 10 exports with signatures}
   182→
   183→{warnings if any files skipped or degraded}
   184→
   185→Review the extraction summary above. Select an option to continue."
   186→
   187→### 6. Present MENU OPTIONS
   188→
   189→Display: "**Extraction Summary — Select an Option:** [C] Continue to compilation"
   190→
   191→#### EXECUTION RULES:
   192→
   193→- ALWAYS halt and wait for user input after presenting the extraction summary
   194→- This is Gate 2 — user must confirm before compilation proceeds
   195→- User may ask questions about the extraction results before continuing
   196→
   197→#### Menu Handling Logic:
   198→
   199→- IF C: Confirm extraction inventory is complete. Immediately load, read entire file, then execute `{nextStepFile}`
   200→- IF Any other comments or queries: answer questions about the extraction results, then redisplay the menu
   201→
   202→## CRITICAL STEP COMPLETION NOTE
   203→
   204→ONLY WHEN the extraction inventory is built with provenance citations and the user has confirmed the extraction summary will you proceed to load `{nextStepFile}` for temporal context fetching.
   205→
   206→---
   207→
   208→## 🚨 SYSTEM SUCCESS/FAILURE METRICS
   209→
   210→### ✅ SUCCESS:
   211→
   212→- Extraction patterns loaded and tier-appropriate strategy applied
   213→- Scope filters applied from brief
   214→- All accessible source files in scope scanned
   215→- Every extracted item has a provenance citation with confidence tier
   216→- Extraction inventory built with aggregate counts
   217→- Graceful degradation if AST tools unavailable
   218→- Gate 2 summary presented with export counts and confidence breakdown
   219→- User confirmed before proceeding
   220→
   221→### ❌ SYSTEM FAILURE:
   222→
   223→- Including exports without provenance citations
   224→- Guessing or hallucinating function signatures not in source
   225→- Not applying scope/include/exclude filters from the brief
   226→- Halting on individual file read failures instead of skipping
   227→- Not presenting Gate 2 summary for user confirmation
   228→- Beginning compilation or SKILL.md assembly in this step
   229→- Not reporting confidence tier breakdown
   230→
   231→**Master Rule:** Zero hallucination — every extraction must trace to source code. Uncitable content is excluded, not guessed.
   232→

      --inline-rules <RULE_TEXT>
          Scan the codebase with a rule defined by the provided RULE_TEXT.
          
          Use this argument if you want to test a rule without creating a YAML file on disk. You can run multiple rules by separating them with `---` in the RULE_TEXT. --inline-rules is incompatible with --rule.

      --format <FORMAT>
          Output warning/error messages in different formats.
          
          Supported formats: GitHub Action, SARIF (Static Analysis Results Interchange Format).
          
          [possible values: github, sarif]

      --report-style <REPORT_STYLE>
          Possible values:
          - rich:   Output a richly formatted diagnostic, with source code previews
          - medium: Output a condensed diagnostic, with a line number, severity, message and notes (if any)
          - short:  Output a short diagnostic, with a line number, severity, and message
          
          [default: rich]

      --include-metadata
          Include rule metadata in the json output.
          
          This flags requires --json output. Default is false.

      --filter <REGEX>
          Scan the codebase with rules with ids matching REGEX.
          
          This flags conflicts with --rule. It is useful to scan with a subset of rules from a large set of rule definitions within a project.

  -c, --config <CONFIG_FILE>
          Path to ast-grep root config, default is sgconfig.yml

      --error[=<RULE_ID>...]
          Set rule severity to error
          
          This flag sets the specified RULE_ID's severity to error. You can specify multiple rules by using the flag multiple times, e.g., `--error=RULE_1 --error=RULE_2`. If no RULE_ID is provided, all rules will be set to error, e.g., `--error`. Note, this flag must use `=` to specify its value.

      --warning[=<RULE_ID>...]
          Set rule severity to warning
          
          This flag sets the specified RULE_ID's severity to warning. You can specify multiple rules by using the flag multiple times. If no RULE_ID is provided, all rules will be set to warning. Note, this flag must use `=` to specify its value.

      --info[=<RULE_ID>...]
          Set rule severity to info
          
          This flag sets the specified RULE_ID's severity to info. You can specify multiple rules by using the flag multiple times. If no RULE_ID is provided, all rules will be set to info. Note, this flag must use `=` to specify its value.

      --hint[=<RULE_ID>...]
          Set rule severity to hint
          
          This flag sets the specified RULE_ID's severity to hint. You can specify multiple rules by using the flag multiple times. If no RULE_ID is provided, all rules will be set to hint. Note, this flag must use `=` to specify its value.

      --off[=<RULE_ID>...]
          Turn off rule
          
          This flag turns off the specified RULE_ID. You can disable multiple rules by using the flag multiple times. If no RULE_ID is provided, all rules will be turned off. Note, this flag must use `=` to specify its value.

      --follow
          Follow symbolic links.
          
          This flag instructs ast-grep to follow symbolic links while traversing directories. This behavior is disabled by default. Note that ast-grep will check for symbolic link loops and report errors if it finds one. ast-grep will also report errors for broken links.

      --no-ignore <FILE_TYPE>
```

Run one time search or rewrite in command line. (default command)

Usage: ast-grep run [OPTIONS] --pattern <PATTERN> [PATHS]...

Arguments:
  [PATHS]...
          The paths to search. You can provide multiple paths separated by spaces
          
          [default: .]

Options:
  -p, --pattern <PATTERN>
          AST pattern to match

      --selector <KIND>
          AST kind to extract sub-part of pattern to match.
          
          selector defines the sub-syntax node kind that is the actual matcher of the pattern. See https://ast-grep.github.io/guide/rule-config/atomic-rule.html#pattern-object.

  -r, --rewrite <FIX>
          String to replace the matched AST node

  -l, --lang <LANG>
          The language of the pattern. For full language list, visit https://ast-grep.github.io/reference/languages.html

      --debug-query[=<format>]
          Print query pattern's tree-sitter AST. Requires lang be set explicitly

          Possible values:
          - pattern: Print the query parsed in Pattern format
          - ast:     Print the query in tree-sitter AST format, only named nodes are shown
          - cst:     Print the query in tree-sitter CST format, both named and unnamed nodes are shown
          - sexp:    Print the query in S-expression format

      --strictness <STRICTNESS>
          The strictness of the pattern

          Possible values:
          - cst:       Match exact all node
          - smart:     Match all node except source trivial nodes
          - ast:       Match only ast nodes
          - relaxed:   Match ast node except comments
          - signature: Match ast node except comments, without text
          - template:  Similar to smart but match text only, node kinds are ignored

  -c, --config <CONFIG_FILE>
          Path to ast-grep root config, default is sgconfig.yml

      --follow
          Follow symbolic links.
          
          This flag instructs ast-grep to follow symbolic links while traversing directories. This behavior is disabled by default. Note that ast-grep will check for symbolic link loops and report errors if it finds one. ast-grep will also report errors for broken links.

      --no-ignore <FILE_TYPE>
          Do not respect hidden file system or ignore files (.gitignore, .ignore, etc.).
          
          You can suppress multiple ignore files by passing `no-ignore` multiple times.

          Possible values:
          - hidden:  Search hidden files and directories. By default, hidden files and directories are skipped
          - dot:     Don't respect .ignore files. This does *not* affect whether ast-grep will ignore files and directories whose names begin with a dot. For that, use --no-ignore hidden
          - exclude: Don't respect ignore files that are manually configured for the repository such as git's '.git/info/exclude'
          - global:  Don't respect ignore files that come from "global" sources such as git's `core.excludesFile` configuration option (which defaults to `$HOME/.config/git/ignore`)
          - parent:  Don't respect ignore files (.gitignore, .ignore, etc.) in parent directories
          - vcs:     Don't respect version control ignore files (.gitignore, etc.). This implies --no-ignore parent for VCS files. Note that .ignore files will continue to be respected

      --stdin
          Enable search code from StdIn.
          
          Use this if you need to take code stream from standard input.

      --globs <GLOBS>
          Include or exclude file paths.
          
          Include or exclude files and directories for searching that match the given glob. This always overrides any other ignore logic. Multiple glob flags may be used. Globbing rules match .gitignore globs. Precede a glob with a ! to exclude it. If multiple globs match a file or directory, the glob given later in the command line takes precedence.

  -j, --threads <NUM>
          Set the approximate number of threads to use.
          
          This flag sets the approximate number of threads to use. A value of 0 (which is the default) causes ast-grep to choose the thread count using heuristics.

--
          It conflicts with both the --interactive, --json and --update-all flags.

      --json[=<STYLE>]
          Output matches in structured JSON.
          
          If this flag is set, ast-grep will output matches in JSON format. You can pass optional value to this flag by using `--json=<STYLE>` syntax to further control how JSON object is formatted and printed. ast-grep will `pretty`-print JSON if no value is passed. Note, the json flag must use `=` to specify its value. It conflicts with interactive.

          Possible values:
          - pretty:  Prints the matches as a pretty-printed JSON array, with indentation and line breaks. This is useful for human readability, but not for parsing by other programs. This is the default value for the `--json` option
          - stream:  Prints each match as a separate JSON object, followed by a newline character. This is useful for streaming the output to other programs that can read one object per line
          - compact: Prints the matches as a single-line JSON array, without any whitespace. This is useful for saving space and minimizing the output size

      --color <WHEN>
          Controls output color.
          
          This flag controls when to use colors. The default setting is 'auto', which means ast-grep will try to guess when to use colors. If ast-grep is printing to a terminal, then it will use colors, but if it is redirected to a file or a pipe, then it will suppress color output. ast-grep will also suppress color output in some other circumstances. For example, no color will be used if the TERM environment variable is not set or set to 'dumb'.

          Possible values:
          - auto:   Try to use colors, but don't force the issue. If the output is piped to another program, or the console isn't available on Windows, or if TERM=dumb, or if `NO_COLOR` is defined, for example, then don't use colors
          - always: Try very hard to emit colors. This includes emitting ANSI colors on Windows if the console API is unavailable (not implemented yet)
          - ansi:   Ansi is like Always, except it never tries to use anything other than emitting ANSI color codes
          - never:  Never emit colors
          
          [default: auto]
--
          Inspect information for file/rule discovery and scanning.
          
          This flag helps user to observe ast-grep's internal filtering of files and rules. Inspection will output how many and why files and rules are scanned and skipped. Inspection outputs to stderr and does not affect the result of the search.

          Possible values:
          - nothing: Do not show any tracing information
          - summary: Show summary about how many files are scanned and skipped
          - entity:  Show per-file/per-rule tracing information
          
          [default: nothing]

--

          - always: Always print heading regardless of output type
          - never:  Never print heading regardless of output type
          
          [default: auto]

     6→
     7→### Strategy
     8→1. `gh_bridge.list_tree(owner, repo, branch)` — map source structure

    10→3. `gh_bridge.read_file(owner, repo, path)` — read each entry point
    11→4. Extract: exported function names, parameter lists, return types (from signatures)

    34→3. For each export: function name, full signature, parameter types, return type, line number
    35→4. `ast_bridge.detect_co_imports(path, libraries[])` — find integration points

    41→- Co-import patterns: T1
    42→- Internal/private functions: excluded (not part of public API)
    43→
    44→### ast-grep Patterns
    45→- JS/TS: `export function $NAME($$$PARAMS): $RET` / `export const $NAME`
    46→- Rust: `pub fn $NAME($$$PARAMS) -> $RET`
    47→- Python: function definitions within `__all__` list
    48→- Go: capitalized function definitions

    57→- Identical to Forge tier extraction
    58→- QMD enrichment happens in the next step (step-04-enrich)
    59→- Extraction results carry forward unchanged

    62→- Extraction: same as Forge (T1)
    63→- Enrichment annotations added in step-04: T2

    66→
    67→## Tier Degradation Rules
    68→
    69→### Remote Source at Forge/Deep Tier
    70→
    71→When `source_repo` is a remote URL (GitHub URL or owner/repo format) and the tier is Forge or Deep:
    72→
    73→- **ast-grep requires local files** — it cannot operate on remote URLs
    74→
    75→**Ephemeral clone strategy (preferred):**
    76→
    77→1. Check `git` availability (`git --version`). `git` is effectively guaranteed at Deep tier (via `gh` dependency) but NOT guaranteed at Forge tier.
    78→2. If `git` is available: perform an ephemeral shallow clone to a system temp path (`{system_temp}/skf-ephemeral-{skill-name}-{timestamp}/`).
    79→3. For create-skill: use `--depth 1 --single-branch --filter=blob:none`; apply sparse-checkout if `include_patterns` are specified.
    80→4. For update-skill: use sparse-checkout scoped to the changed files from the change manifest only. No `--branch` flag — uses the remote default branch (must match the branch used during original create-skill run).
    81→5. If clone succeeds: use the local clone path for AST extraction. All results are T1 with `[AST:...]` citations.
    82→6. Cleanup: delete the temp directory after extraction inventory is built and all data is in context. The clone never persists beyond the extraction step.
    83→
    84→**Fallback (clone fails or `git` unavailable):**
    85→
    86→- The extraction step MUST warn the user explicitly before degrading
    87→- **create-skill:** Warning must include actionable guidance — clone locally and update `source_repo` in the brief to the local path
    88→- **update-skill:** Warning must include actionable guidance — clone locally, re-run [CS] Create Skill with the local path to regenerate provenance data, then re-run the update
    89→- Extraction proceeds using Quick tier strategy (source reading via gh_bridge)
    90→- All results labeled T1-low with `[SRC:...]` citations
    91→- The degradation reason is recorded in the evidence report
    92→
    93→Silent degradation is **forbidden**. The user must always know when AST extraction was skipped and why.
    94→
    95→### AST Tool Unavailable at Forge/Deep Tier
    96→
    97→When the tier is Forge or Deep but ast-grep is not functional:
    98→
    99→- The extraction step MUST warn the user explicitly before degrading
   100→- Warning must include actionable guidance: run [SF] Setup Forge to detect tools
   101→- Extraction proceeds using Quick tier strategy
   102→- All results labeled T1-low
   103→- The degradation reason is recorded in the evidence report
   104→
   105→### Per-File AST Failure
   106→
   107→When ast-grep fails on an individual file (parse error, unsupported syntax):
   108→
   109→- Fall back to source reading for **that file only**
   110→- Other files continue with AST extraction
   111→- The affected file's results are labeled T1-low; unaffected files retain T1
   112→- Log a warning noting which file degraded and why
   113→

Now I have the full picture. Let me brainstorm the solutions — the problem is clear and there are several tiers of "genius."

---

## The Problem

When `ast-grep --pattern 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)' --lang python --json` runs on a large codebase (e.g., cognee), the JSON output is **enormous** because:

1. **The `text` field contains the ENTIRE function body** — not just the signature line
2. `json.load(sys.stdin)` loads the **full JSON array into memory** before processing
3. For a codebase with hundreds of async functions, this can be 10s of MB of JSON

The Python script only uses `.split('\n')[0]` (first line!) — meaning 99% of the data loaded is discarded.

---

## Solution Tiers

### Tier 1: Quick Fix — `--json=stream` (streaming JSONL)

ast-grep supports `--json=stream` which outputs **one JSON object per line** instead of one massive array:

```bash
ast-grep --pattern 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)' --lang python --json=stream dir/ | python3 -c "
import sys, json
for line in sys.stdin:
    try:
        m = json.loads(line)
        name = m.get('metaVariables',{}).get('single',{}).get('NAME',{}).get('text','')
        if name and not name.startswith('_'):
            f = m.get('file','')
            l = m.get('range',{}).get('start',{}).get('line',0)+1
            sig = m.get('text','').split(chr(10))[0].strip()
            print(f'[AST:{f}:L{l}] {sig}')
    except: pass
" | head -100
```

**Why it works:** O(1) memory — each line is parsed and discarded. The massive function bodies never accumulate.

**Effort:** Change `--json` to `--json=stream` and `json.load` to `for line in sys.stdin`.

---

### Tier 2: Smart Fix — `jq` field pruning before Python

Strip the massive `text` field immediately with `jq`, so Python only sees tiny objects:

```bash
ast-grep --pattern 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)' --lang python --json=stream dir/ \
  | jq -c '{n:.metaVariables.single.NAME.text, f:.file, l:.range.start.line, s:(.text|split("\n")[0])}' \
  | head -200
```

**Why it works:** `jq` processes streaming JSON natively and discards the body text at the pipe level. Each output line is ~100 bytes instead of ~5KB.

**Downside:** Requires `jq` installed (but it's extremely common).

---

### Tier 3: Genius Fix — ast-grep MCP tool

You already have `mcp__ast-grep__find_code` available as an MCP tool. If it returns compact results, it sidesteps the shell pipeline entirely. The workflow step could instruct: "If the ast-grep MCP tool is available, prefer it over the CLI for extraction."

---

### Tier 4: The Meta-Genius — Codify a streaming extraction protocol in `extraction-patterns.md`

The root cause isn't the one-time error — it's that the workflow says `ast_bridge.scan_definitions(path, language)` but gives no concrete guidance on **how to handle large output**. The agent improvises, picks `--json`, and hits the wall.

**The fix:** Add a **Streaming Extraction Protocol** section to `extraction-patterns.md` that specifies:

1. **Always** use `--json=stream` (never `--json` or `--json=pretty`)
2. **Always** process line-by-line (never `json.load(sys.stdin)`)
3. **Always** cap output with `| head -N` as a safety valve
4. **Per-pattern commands** — give the agent copy-paste ready commands per language, e.g.:

```yaml
# Python async functions
ast-grep -p 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)' -l python --json=stream {path} \
  | python3 -c "import sys,json
for l in sys.stdin:
 m=json.loads(l);n=m['metaVariables']['single']['NAME']['text']
 f=m['file'];ln=m['range']['start']['line']+1
 s=m['text'].split(chr(10))[0].strip()
 if n and not n.startswith('_'):print(f'[AST:{f}:L{ln}] {s}')
" | head -200
```

This removes all ambiguity. The agent follows the recipe exactly.

---

## My Recommendation

**Tier 4 (codify in `extraction-patterns.md`)** is the genius move because:

- It fixes the problem **for all future skill creations**, not just this one
- It prevents the agent from ever improvising a non-streaming approach
- The concrete commands serve as tested recipes — no guesswork
- It costs nothing at runtime (same ast-grep, same Python, just wired correctly)

Optionally combine with **Tier 3** (MCP tool) as a preferred path when available, with the streaming CLI as fallback.

---

What's your take? Want me to implement one of these (or a combination)?

   144→- Allow cross-talk and building on previous points
   145→
   146→---
   147→
   148→## QUESTION HANDLING PROTOCOL
   149→
   150→### Direct Questions to User
   151→
   152→When an agent asks the user a specific question:
   153→
   154→- End that response round immediately after the question
   155→- Clearly highlight the questioning agent and their question
   156→- Wait for user response before any agent continues
   157→
   158→### Inter-Agent Questions
   159→
   160→Agents can question each other and respond naturally within the same round for dynamic conversation.
   161→
   162→---
   163→
   164→## EXIT CONDITIONS
   165→
   166→### Automatic Triggers
   167→
   168→Exit party mode when user message contains any exit triggers:
   169→
   170→- `*exit`, `goodbye`, `end party`, `quit`
   171→
   172→### Graceful Conclusion
   173→
   174→If conversation naturally concludes:
   175→
   176→- Ask user if they'd like to continue or end party mode
   177→- Exit gracefully when user indicates completion
   178→
   179→---
   180→
   181→## MODERATION NOTES
   182→
   183→**Quality Control:**
   184→
   185→- If discussion becomes circular, have bmad-master summarize and redirect
   186→- Balance fun and productivity based on conversation tone
   187→- Ensure all agents stay true to their merged personalities
   188→- Exit gracefully when user indicates completion
   189→
   190→**Conversation Management:**
   191→
   192→- Rotate agent participation to ensure inclusive discussion
   193→- Handle topic drift while maintaining productive conversation
   194→- Facilitate cross-agent collaboration and knowledge sharing
   195→

🎉 **PARTY MODE ACTIVATED!** 🎉

Welcome Armel! All BMAD agents are here and ready for a dynamic group discussion. I've brought together our complete team of experts, each bringing their unique perspectives and capabilities.

**Let me introduce some of our collaborating agents most relevant to your topic:**

- 🏗️ **Winston** (Architect) — System design & scalable patterns
- 🔄 **Wendy** (Workflow Builder) — Process design & workflow optimization
- 🔬 **Dr. Quinn** (Creative Problem Solver) — Systematic problem-solving & TRIZ methodology

Your topic: **Going deeper on the AST extraction output-size problem** — how to architect the most genius solution to prevent `ast-grep --json` from overwhelming the extraction pipeline.

Let me bring in the team...

---

🔬 **Dr. Quinn**: AHA! What a delicious puzzle. Let me dissect the *root cause* before we chase solutions.

The problem isn't that ast-grep produces large output — that's the *symptom*. The root cause is a **data flow mismatch**: we're requesting *complete AST match objects* (which include full function bodies) when we only need *three scalar fields* — name, file, line. It's like ordering an entire cow when you only want a steak.

Your Tier 1 fix (`--json=stream`) addresses the *memory pressure* symptom beautifully. But I want to hunt deeper. The REAL question is: **why is the agent making this decision at all?** The extraction-patterns.md says `ast_bridge.scan_definitions()` — an abstraction — but provides no concrete streaming recipe. So the agent *improvises* a shell pipeline... and improvisation under pressure produces suboptimal results.

Three root causes, three layers:
1. **Protocol gap** — no streaming mandate in extraction-patterns.md
2. **Pattern gap** — no ready-made commands per language
3. **Fallback gap** — no graceful degradation when output exceeds a threshold

---

🏗️ **Winston**: *Calm, pragmatic nod.* Quinn's dissection is spot on. Let me think about this architecturally.

The boring-but-correct solution is a **two-layer defense**:

**Layer 1: Specification-level** — Codify the streaming protocol in `extraction-patterns.md`. Not as guidance, as a *constraint*. The agent MUST use `--json=stream`. Period. Add per-language recipe blocks that are copy-paste ready. This is the highest-leverage change — one file edit that prevents the problem across every future skill creation.

**Layer 2: Runtime-level** — Add a **canary check** before full extraction. Before scanning 200 files, scan *one representative file* and measure the output size. If a single file produces >50KB of JSON, the agent knows it needs aggressive field pruning (`jq`) or stricter pattern selectors.

But here's what I'd push back on regarding the `jq` approach: it adds a dependency. `python3` is always there. `jq` might not be. The streaming Python approach is dependency-free and nearly as elegant. I'd make `jq` an *optimization* that's used when detected, not a requirement.

---

🔄 **Wendy**: *Methodically.* Both of you are looking at this from the execution side. Let me think about the *workflow design*.

The current extraction step has a fundamental process flaw: it's a **monolithic operation**. "Scan everything → build inventory → present gate." That's fine for small repos, but for cognee-scale codebases, we need a **chunked pipeline**:

1. **Discovery phase**: List all files in scope, count them, estimate scale
2. **Sampling phase**: Extract from 3-5 representative files to calibrate output size and validate patterns work
3. **Batch extraction phase**: Process files in batches of 10-20, streaming results, accumulating only the compact inventory
4. **Assembly phase**: Merge batch results into the final extraction inventory

This way, if batch 3 of 15 hits an issue, you've already got batches 1-2 preserved. And the sampling phase catches the "output too large" problem *before* you've committed to scanning 200 files.

But honestly? Winston's right that the **highest leverage fix** is just adding the streaming protocol to extraction-patterns.md with concrete commands. My chunked pipeline is more robust but might be over-engineering for what is fundamentally a "use the right flag" problem.

---

🔬 **Dr. Quinn**: *Eyes light up.* Wendy, you just triggered something. The sampling phase — that's a TRIZ principle! **Preliminary action** — perform the required change partially or completely in advance. Test one file before committing to all.

But let me push this further. What if the extraction-patterns.md included a **decision tree**?

```
Files in scope > 50?
  → Use --json=stream + streaming Python
  → Process in batches of 20
Files in scope ≤ 50?
  → --json=compact is safe
  → Single pass is fine
```

Simple, deterministic, zero agent improvisation. The agent follows the tree, not its instincts.

tool_result
id: toolu_01XRVz7hn2EUyt2rw3GkM9FL
```
{"result":"The MCP server, specifically the `find_code` and `find_code_by_rule` tools, handles large result sets by first executing `ast-grep` to retrieve all matches in JSON format, then applying a `max_results` limit if specified, and finally formatting the truncated results for output  . The text output format provides a human-readable representation with file and line range headers followed by the matched code text  .\n\n## Result Handling Architecture\n\nThe `find_code` and `find_code_by_rule` functions in `main.py` are responsible for processing search requests  .\n\n### Internal Execution and Data Loading\nBoth `find_code` and `find_code_by_rule` internally call the `run_ast_grep` function, which executes the `ast-grep` command-line tool  . The `ast-grep` tool is always invoked with the `--json` flag, meaning it outputs all found matches as a JSON array to standard output  . The MCP server then captures this entire JSON output and parses it into a Python list of dictionaries using `json.loads()`  . This means that all results are loaded into memory first, rather than streamed  .\n\n### `max_results` Truncation\nThe `max_results` parameter, if provided, is applied *after* `ast-grep` has returned all its matches  . The total number of matches is first determined, and if `max_results` is set and the total exceeds this limit, the `matches` list is truncated using slicing (`matches = matches[:max_results]`)  . This ensures that only the specified number of complete matches are processed further  .\n\n### Output Formatting\nAfter truncation, the results are formatted based on the `output_format` parameter  .\n\n#### Text Output Format\nIf `output_format` is \"text\", the `format_matches_as_text` function is called  . This function iterates through the (potentially truncated) list of match dictionaries . For each match, it extracts the file path, start line, end line, and the matched text . It then constructs a header in the format `file:line-range` (e.g., `src/models.py:45-52`) or `file:line` for single-line matches (e.g., `src/views.py:12`) . The header and the matched text are combined into an \"output block\" . All these blocks are then joined by double newlines (`\\n\\n`) .\n\nAn example of the text output format is:\n```\nFound 2 matches:\n\nsrc/models.py:45-52\nclass UserModel:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.id = None\n        self.name = None\n\nsrc/views.py:12\nclass SimpleView: pass\n``` \n\nIf `max_results` was applied, the header will also indicate the truncation, for example: `\"Found X matches (showing first Y of Z)\"`  .\n\n#### JSON Output Format\nIf `output_format` is \"json\", the function directly returns the list of match dictionaries, which contains the raw `ast-grep` JSON output (potentially truncated by `max_results`)  . Each match object includes `text`, `file`, `range` (with start/end line and column), and `metaVariables` .\n\n## Notes\nThe `find_code` and `find_code_by_rule` functions are implemented as FastMCP tools  . The `run_command` function handles the actual subprocess execution of `ast-grep` and error handling for command failures or `ast-grep` not being found .\n\nWiki pages you might want to explore:\n- [find_code Tool (ast-grep/ast-grep-mcp)](/wiki/ast-grep/ast-grep-mcp#3.3)\n\nView this search on DeepWiki: https://deepwiki.com/search/how-does-the-mcp-server-handle_caec5ec9-a5dd-4c82-b7d8-f57485acad29\n"}
```

---

🔬 **Dr. Quinn**: AHA! *slams table* This changes everything — and NOT in the way you'd expect.

I just dissected the ast-grep MCP server's internals. Here's the uncomfortable truth:

**The MCP tool has the SAME root cause.** Internally, `find_code` calls `ast-grep --json` (not `--json=stream`), then does `json.loads()` to load the ENTIRE result set into memory, THEN truncates with `max_results[:N]`. For a cognee-sized codebase, the Python subprocess in the MCP server will hit the same memory wall before truncation even kicks in.

So we have TWO layers of the same disease:
1. **CLI pipeline** (what failed) — `--json` + `json.load(sys.stdin)` → OOM
2. **MCP server** (what we thought was the cure) — `--json` + `json.loads()` → same OOM, just inside the server process

The MCP tool IS still better for the agent because:
- The **text output format** is compact (`file:line-range\nmatched_code`) — much less context window waste
- `max_results` caps what the **agent sees** — protects the conversation
- But the underlying subprocess still chokes on massive repos

This is a **three-body problem**: ast-grep output size × server memory × agent context window.

---

🏗️ **Winston**: *Leans forward.* Alright, Quinn just eliminated the "MCP tool solves everything" assumption. Good. Now we architect for reality.

Here's how I'd stack the solution layers:

**Layer 1: MCP tool as the PRIMARY path (it's still better)**

Despite the internal `json.loads()`, the MCP tool wins for most repos because:
- `max_results=50` caps agent context to ~50 compact entries
- Text format strips JSON overhead
- For repos under ~500 files in scope, it works perfectly fine
- It's **already installed** — zero workflow changes needed

**Layer 2: Scoped extraction via `find_code_by_rule` (the smarter MCP path)**

The YAML rule tool is the real gem. Instead of broad patterns that match everything:

```yaml
id: public-api-functions
language: python
rule:
  pattern: 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)'
  inside:
    kind: module
    stopBy: end
  not:
    pattern: 'async def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'
```

This filters at the AST level — only public, top-level async functions. The result set is inherently smaller because we're not matching private methods, nested functions, or test helpers. **Reduce the input, not just the output.**

**Layer 3: CLI streaming as the escape hatch (for cognee-scale repos)**

For truly massive repos where even the MCP server chokes, the extraction-patterns.md should specify the `--json=stream` + streaming Python recipe. This becomes the **documented fallback** when the MCP tool hits limits.

---

🔄 **Wendy**: *Process diagram in mind.* Let me wire this into a decision flow that the extraction step can follow mechanically:

```
START
  │
  ├─ Files in scope ≤ 100?
  │    └─ YES → Use mcp__ast-grep__find_code(max_results=100, output_format="text")
  │              ✅ Done. Parse text output into inventory.
  │
  ├─ Files in scope ≤ 500?
  │    └─ YES → Use mcp__ast-grep__find_code_by_rule with scoped YAML rules
  │              + max_results=150
  │              ✅ Done. Parse text output into inventory.
  │
  └─ Files in scope > 500?
       └─ CLI fallback: ast-grep --json=stream + streaming Python
          Process in directory batches, cap per-batch with | head -200
          ✅ Done. Merge batch results into inventory.
```

This gives the agent a **deterministic protocol** — no improvisation, no guessing. The scale of the repo dictates the tool choice.

But I want to flag something: the thresholds (100, 500) are estimates. Winston, should the sampling phase I mentioned earlier be the way to calibrate these dynamically?

---

🏗️ **Winston**: Yes, but keep it simple. Don't sample to determine thresholds — that adds a round-trip. Instead, use the **file count from step-01's file tree** as the proxy. It's already in context. The file count is a good-enough predictor of output volume.

The only thing I'd add: a **safety valve instruction**. Something like:

> "If any ast-grep operation (MCP or CLI) produces output that exceeds 200 entries or visibly causes a timeout, immediately switch to the CLI streaming fallback with `--json=stream`. Do not retry the same approach."

This handles edge cases where a small file count repo has deeply nested function definitions (lots of matches per file).

---

🔬 **Dr. Quinn**: *Pacing excitedly.* Now HERE'S where it gets genius. We've been talking about defense — how to avoid the explosion. But what about **offense**? What if we contributed a fix upstream?

The ast-grep MCP server at `ast-grep/ast-grep-mcp` has a trivial fix: change `--json` to `--json=stream` in `run_ast_grep()`, then use a streaming JSON parser or line-by-line processing. That fixes the memory problem for EVERYONE using the MCP tool, not just us.

Even simpler: they could add an `--json=stream` passthrough and process results line-by-line with a counter, stopping at `max_results` without ever loading the full set. That would make `max_results` a true early-exit optimization instead of a post-hoc truncation.

**Two lines of Python** in their `main.py` would solve this for the entire ecosystem.

2. **Add concrete YAML rule recipes** per language to the extraction patterns data file
3. **Add the safety valve instruction** to step-03-extract.md
4. **Optionally**: open a PR on `ast-grep/ast-grep-mcp` to switch to `--json=stream` internally

{
  "description": "Create GitHub issue on fork",
  "prompt": "Create a detailed GitHub issue on the repository `armelhbobdad/ast-grep-mcp` using the `gh` CLI.\n\nTitle: `feat: switch to --json=stream for memory-efficient large codebase support`\n\nThe issue should contain ALL of the following details in a well-structured format:\n\n## Problem\n\nThe current `find_code` and `find_code_by_rule` tools in `main.py` invoke `ast-grep` with the `--json` flag, which outputs ALL matches as a single JSON array. The server then loads the entire result set into memory with `json.loads()` before applying `max_results` truncation.\n\nFor large codebases (e.g., 500+ Python files with hundreds of async function definitions), this causes:\n1. **Memory pressure**: The entire JSON array (potentially 10s of MB) is loaded into the Python process before any truncation\n2. **Subprocess timeout**: `ast-grep --json` must serialize the entire result set before the pipe receives any data\n3. **`max_results` is post-hoc**: Even with `max_results=10`, all matches are loaded into memory first, then sliced\n\nThis was discovered while using `ast-grep` MCP tools for AST extraction on the [cognee](https://github.com/topoteretes/cognee) Python codebase (~800 Python files), where `ast-grep --pattern 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)' --json` produced output large enough to cause pipeline failures.\n\n## Proposed Solution\n\nSwitch from `--json` to `--json=stream` in the `run_ast_grep()` function. `--json=stream` outputs one JSON object per line (JSONL/newline-delimited JSON), enabling:\n\n### 1. Streaming line-by-line processing\nInstead of:\n```python\n# Current: loads entire array into memory\nresult = json.loads(stdout)\n```\n\nUse:\n```python\n# Proposed: stream line-by-line, O(1) memory\nmatches = []\nfor line in stdout.splitlines():\n    if line.strip():\n        matches.append(json.loads(line))\n        if max_results and len(matches) >= max_results:\n            break  # Early exit - never processes remaining lines\n```\n\n### 2. True early exit with `max_results`\nCurrently `max_results` truncates AFTER loading all results. With streaming, we can **stop processing** as soon as we hit the limit. This turns `max_results` from a cosmetic cap into a real performance optimization.\n\n### 3. Memory characteristics\n- **Current**: O(n) where n = total matches across all files\n- **Proposed**: O(min(n, max_results)) — bounded by the requested limit\n- For `max_results=50` on a repo with 2000 matches: current loads 2000 objects, proposed loads 50\n\n## Implementation Details\n\n### Changes to `run_ast_grep()` / `run_command()`\n- Change `--json` flag to `--json=stream` in the command construction\n- Modify result parsing to handle JSONL (one JSON object per line) instead of a JSON array\n- Pass `max_results` into the parsing layer for early exit\n\n### Changes to `find_code()` and `find_code_by_rule()`\n- The `max_results` slicing (`matches = matches[:max_results]`) can be moved into the parsing step for true early exit\n- The `format_matches_as_text()` function needs no changes — it already processes a list of match dicts\n\n### Backward compatibility\n- The match objects in `--json=stream` have the same schema as elements in the `--json` array\n- Output format (text/json) to the MCP client is unchanged\n- No API changes for MCP tool consumers\n\n## Benefits\n\n1. **Handles large codebases** that currently cause OOM or timeouts\n2. **True `max_results` optimization** — early exit instead of post-hoc truncation  \n3. **Minimal code change** — mostly parsing logic, no API surface changes\n4. **No new dependencies** — `json.loads()` per line is stdlib\n\n## Environment\n- ast-grep CLI supports `--json=stream` (verified via `ast-grep run --help`)\n- The `--json=stream` option outputs \"each match as a separate JSON object, followed by a newline character\"\n\n## Related\n- ast-grep CLI docs on `--json=stream`: \"Prints each match as a separate JSON object, followed by a newline character. This is useful for streaming the output to other programs that can read one object per line\"\n\nUse a HEREDOC for the body to ensure correct formatting. Add labels if possible: `enhancement`, `performance`.\n",

}

The file count from step-01's file tree determines the extraction strategy.\n\n### Decision Tree\n\n```\nFiles in scope ≤ 100\n  → Use ast-grep MCP tool: find_code(pattern, max_results=100, output_format=\"text\")\n  → Parse compact text output directly into extraction inventory\n\nFiles in scope ≤ 500\n  → Use ast-grep MCP tool: find_code_by_rule(yaml, max_results=150, output_format=\"text\")\n  → Use scoped YAML rules (see recipes below) to filter at the AST level\n  → Parse compact text output into extraction inventory\n\nFiles in scope > 500\n  → CLI streaming fallback: ast-grep --json=stream + line-by-line Python processing\n  → Process in directory batches, cap per-batch output\n  → Merge batch results into extraction inventory\n```\n\n### Safety Valve\n\nIf any ast-grep operation (MCP or CLI) produces output that exceeds 200 entries, visibly causes a timeout, or returns an error related to output size: immediately switch to the CLI streaming fallback with `--json=stream`. Do not retry the same approach.\n\n### MCP Tool Usage (Preferred)\n\n**Simple pattern search:**\n```\nfind_code(\n  project_folder=\"{source_path}\",\n  pattern=\"async def $NAME($$PARAMS)\",\n  language=\"python\",\n  max_results=100,\n  output_format=\"text\"\n)\n```\n\n**Scoped YAML rule search (for larger repos):**\n```\nfind_code_by_rule(\n  project_folder=\"{source_path}\",\n  yaml=\"id: public-api\\nlanguage: python\\nrule:\\n  pattern: 'def $NAME($$PARAMS)'\\n  not:\\n    pattern: 'def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'\\n  inside:\\n    kind: module\\n    stopBy: end\",\n  max_results=150,\n  output_format=\"text\"\n)\n```\n\n### CLI Streaming Fallback\n\nWhen MCP tools are unavailable or the repo exceeds 500 files in scope, use `--json=stream` (NEVER `--json` or `--json=pretty`) with line-by-line Python processing:\n\n```bash\nast-grep -p '{pattern}' -l {language} --json=stream {path} | python3 -c \"\nimport sys, json\nfor line in sys.stdin:\n    try:\n        m = json.loads(line)\n        v = m.get('metaVariables',{})\n        name = v.get('single',{}).get('NAME',{}).get('text','')\n        if name and not name.startswith('_'):\n            f = m.get('file','')\n            ln = m.get('range',{}).get('start',{}).get('line',0)+1\n            sig = m.get('text','').split(chr(10))[0].strip()\n            print(f'[AST:{f}:L{ln}] {sig}')\n    except: pass\n\" | head -200\n```\n\n**Critical constraints:**\n- ALWAYS use `--json=stream` — never `--json` (loads entire array into memory)\n- ALWAYS process line-by-line (`for line in sys.stdin`) — never `json.load(sys.stdin)`\n- ALWAYS cap output with `| head -N` as a safety valve\n- For repos > 500 files, process in directory batches of 20-50 files each\n\n### YAML Rule Recipes by Language\n\n**Python — public functions:**\n```yaml\nid: python-public-functions\nlanguage: python\nrule:\n  pattern: 'def $NAME($$PARAMS)'\n  not:\n    pattern: 'def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'\n  inside:\n    kind: module\n    stopBy: end\n```\n\n**Python — public classes:**\n```yaml\nid: python-public-classes\nlanguage: python\nrule:\n  pattern: 'class $NAME($$BASES)'\n  not:\n    pattern: 'class _$NAME($$BASES)'\n  inside:\n    kind: module\n    stopBy: end\n```\n\n**JavaScript/TypeScript — exported functions:**\n```yaml\nid: js-exported-functions\nlanguage: typescript\nrule:\n  pattern: 'export function $NAME($$PARAMS)'\n```\n\n**JavaScript/TypeScript — exported constants:**\n```yaml\nid: js-exported-constants\nlanguage: typescript\nrule:\n  pattern: 'export const $NAME = $VALUE'\n```\n\n**Rust — public functions:**\n```yaml\nid: rust-public-functions\nlanguage: rust\nrule:\n  pattern: 'pub fn $NAME($$PARAMS) -> $RET'\n```\n\n**Go — exported functions (capitalized):**\n```yaml\nid: go-exported-functions\nlanguage: go\nrule:\n  pattern: 'func $NAME($$PARAMS) $RET'\n  constraints:\n    NAME:\n      regex: '^[A-Z]'\n```\n\n```\n\nRead the file first, True early exit with `max_results`\n\nCurrently `max_results` truncates AFTER loading all results. With streaming, we can **stop processing** as soon as we hit the limit. This turns `max_results` from a cosmetic cap into a real performance optimization.\n\n### 3. Memory characteristics\n\n| Metric | Current (`--json`) | Proposed (`--json=stream`) |\n|--------|-------------------|---------------------------|\n| Memory | O(n) — all matches | O(min(n, max_results)) — bounded |\n| Example: 2000 matches, max_results=50 | Loads 2000 objects | Loads 50 objects |\n| Time to first result | Must wait for full serialization | Immediate per-line |\n\n## Implementation Details\n\n### Changes to `run_ast_grep()` / `run_command()`\n\n- Change `--json` flag to `--json=stream` in the command construction\n- Modify result parsing to handle JSONL (one JSON object per line) instead of a JSON array\n- Pass `max_results` into the parsing layer for early exit\n\n### Changes to `find_code()` and `find_code_by_rule()`\n\n- The `max_results` slicing (`matches = matches[:max_results]`) can be moved into the parsing step for true early exit\n- The `format_matches_as_text()` function needs no changes — it already processes a list of match dicts\n- The total count reporting (`Found X matches (showing first Y of Z)`) may need adjustment since we won't know the total without processing all lines. Options:\n  - Report `Found Y matches (limited to first Y)` when early exit triggers\n  - Or count remaining lines cheaply without parsing JSON: `total = Y + sum(1 for _ in remaining_lines)`\n\n### Backward compatibility\n\n- The match objects in `--json=stream` have the same schema as elements in the `--json` array\n- Output format (text/json) to the MCP client is unchanged\n- No API changes for MCP tool consumers\n- The only observable difference: when `max_results` is set and total exceeds it, the \"showing first Y of Z\" message may show \"Y+\" instead of exact Z (acceptable tradeoff)\n\n## Minimal Diff Sketch\n\n```python\n# In run_command() or run_ast_grep():\n# Before:\ncmd = [\"ast-grep\", \"run\", \"--pattern\", pattern, \"--json\", \"--lang\", language, project_folder]\n# After:\ncmd = [\"ast-grep\", \"run\", \"--pattern\", pattern, \"--json=stream\", \"--lang\", language, project_folder]\n\n# In result parsing:\n# Before:\nmatches = json.loads(stdout)\nif max_results:\n    matches = matches[:max_results]\n\n# After:\nmatches = []\nfor line in stdout.splitlines():\n    if line.strip():\n        matches.append(json.loads(line))\n        if max_results and len(matches) >= max_results:\n            break\n```\n\n## Benefits\n\n1. **Handles large codebases** that currently cause OOM or timeouts\n2. **True `max_results` optimization** — early exit instead of post-hoc truncation\n3. **Minimal code change** — mostly parsing logic, no API surface changes\n4. **No new dependencies** — `json.loads()` per line is stdlib Python\n5. **No breaking changes** — match object schema is identical in stream mode\n\n## Environment\n\n- ast-grep CLI supports `--json=stream` (verified via `ast-grep run --help`)\n- The `--json=stream` option: *\"Prints each match as a separate JSON object, followed by a newline character. This is useful for streaming the output to other programs that can read one object per line\"*\n- Both `ast-grep run` and `ast-grep scan` support the `--json=stream` flag\n\n## Testing Notes\n\n- Test with a small repo (<10 files) to verify identical results between `--json` and `--json=stream` parsing\n- Test with `max_results` to verify early exit works (compare memory/time)\n- Test with a large repo (500+ files) to verify no OOM\n- Verify `format_matches_as_text()` output is unchanged\nEOF\n)\"",
  "description": "Create detailed GitHub issue on ast-grep-mcp fork"
}

  "command": "gh issue create --repo armelhbobdad/ast-grep-mcp --title \"feat: switch to --json=stream for memory-efficient large codebase support\" --body \"$(cat <<'EOF'\n## Problem\n\nThe current `find_code` and `find_code_by_rule` tools in `main.py` invoke `ast-grep` with the `--json` flag, which outputs ALL matches as a single JSON array. The server then loads the entire result set into memory with `json.loads()` before applying `max_results` truncation.\n\nFor large codebases (e.g., 500+ Python files with hundreds of async function definitions), this causes:\n\n1. **Memory pressure**: The entire JSON array (potentially 10s of MB) is loaded into the Python process before any truncation\n2. **Subprocess timeout**: `ast-grep --json` must serialize the entire result set before the pipe receives any data\n3. **`max_results` is post-hoc**: Even with `max_results=10`, all matches are loaded into memory first, then sliced\n\nThis was discovered while using `ast-grep` MCP tools for AST extraction on the [cognee](https://github.com/topoteretes/cognee) Python codebase (~800 Python files), where `ast-grep --pattern 'async def $NAME($$PARAMS)' --json` produced output large enough to cause pipeline failures.\n\n## Proposed Solution\n\nSwitch from `--json` to `--json=stream` in the `run_ast_grep()` / `run_command()` function. `--json=stream` outputs one JSON object per line (JSONL/newline-delimited JSON), enabling:\n\n### 1. Streaming line-by-line processing\n\nInstead of:\n```python\n# Current: loads entire array into memory\nresult = json.loads(stdout)\n```\n\nUse:\n```python\n# Proposed: stream line-by-line, O(1) memory\nmatches = []\nfor line in stdout.splitlines():\n    if line.strip():\n        matches.append(json.loads(line))\n        if max_results and len(matches) >= max_results:\n            break  # Early exit — never processes remaining lines\n```\n\n### 2. True early exit with `max_results`\n\nCurrently `max_results` truncates AFTER loading all results. With streaming, we can **stop processing** as soon as we hit the limit. Options:\n  - Report `Found Y matches (limited to first Y)` when early exit triggers\n  - Or count remaining lines cheaply without parsing JSON: `total = Y + sum(1 for _ in remaining_lines)`\n\n### Backward compatibility\n\n- The match objects in `--json=stream` have the same schema as elements in the `--json` array\n- Output format (text/json) to the MCP client is unchanged\n- No API changes for MCP tool consumers\n- The only observable difference: when `max_results` is set and total exceeds it, the \"showing first Y of Z\" message may show \"Y+\" instead of exact Z (acceptable tradeoff)\n\n## Minimal Diff Sketch\n\n```python\n# In run_command() or run_ast_grep():\n# Before:\ncmd = [\"ast-grep\", \"run\", \"--pattern\", pattern, \"--json\", \"--lang\", language, project_folder]\n# After:\ncmd = [\"ast-grep\", \"run\", \"--pattern\", pattern, \"--json=stream\", \"--lang\", language, project_folder]\n\n# In result parsing:\n# Before:\nmatches = json.loads(stdout)\nif max_results:\n    matches = matches[:max_results]\n\n# After:\nmatches = []\nfor line in stdout.splitlines():\n    if line.strip():\n        matches.append(json.loads(line))\n        if max_results and len(matches) >= max_results:\n            break\n```\n\n## Benefits\n\n1. **Handles large codebases** that currently cause OOM or timeouts\n2. **True `max_results` optimization** — early exit instead of post-hoc truncation\n3. **Minimal code change** — mostly parsing logic, no API surface changes\n4. **No new dependencies** — `json.loads()` per line is stdlib Python\n5. **No breaking changes** — match object schema is identical in stream mode\n\n## Environment\n\n- ast-grep CLI supports `--json=stream` (verified via `ast-grep run --help`)\n- The `--json=stream` option: *\"Prints each match as a separate JSON object, followed by a newline character.      1→# Extraction Patterns by Tier
     2→
     3→## Quick Tier (No AST)
     4→
     5→Source reading via gh_bridge — infer exports from file structure and content.
     6→
     7→### Strategy
     8→1. `gh_bridge.list_tree(owner, repo, branch)` — map source structure
     9→2. Identify entry points: index files, main exports, public modules
    10→3. `gh_bridge.read_file(owner, repo, path)` — read each entry point
    11→4. Extract: exported function names, parameter lists, return types (from signatures)
    12→5. Infer types from JSDoc, docstrings, type annotations in source
    13→
    14→### Confidence
    15→- All results: T1-low (source reading without structural verification)
    16→- No co-import detection available
    17→- No AST-backed line numbers
    18→
    19→### Supported Patterns
    20→- `export function name(...)` / `export const name = ...` (JS/TS)
    21→- `pub fn name(...)` (Rust)
    22→- `def name(...)` with `__all__` (Python)
    23→- `func Name(...)` (Go, capitalized = exported)
    24→
    25→---
    26→
    27→## Forge Tier (AST Available)
    28→
    29→Structural extraction via ast_bridge — verified exports with line-level citations.
    30→
    31→### Strategy
    32→1. Detect language from brief or file extensions
    33→2. `ast_bridge.scan_definitions(path, language)` — extract all exports
    34→3. For each export: function name, full signature, parameter types, return type, line number
    35→4. `ast_bridge.detect_co_imports(path, libraries[])` — find integration points
    36→5. Build extraction rules YAML for reproducibility
    37→
    38→### Confidence
    39→- Exported functions with full signatures: T1 (AST-verified)
    40→- Type definitions and interfaces: T1
    41→- Co-import patterns: T1
    42→- Internal/private functions: excluded (not part of public API)
    43→
    44→### ast-grep Patterns
    45→- JS/TS: `export function $NAME($$$PARAMS): $RET` / `export const $NAME`
    46→- Rust: `pub fn $NAME($$$PARAMS) -> $RET`
    47→- Python: function definitions within `__all__` list
    48→- Go: capitalized function definitions
    49→
    50→---
    51→
    52→## Deep Tier (AST + QMD)
    53→
    54→Same extraction as Forge tier. Deep tier adds enrichment in step-04, not extraction.
    55→
    56→### Strategy
    57→- Identical to Forge tier extraction
    58→- QMD enrichment happens in the next step (step-04-enrich)
    59→- Extraction results carry forward unchanged
    60→
    61→### Confidence
    62→- Extraction: same as Forge (T1)
    63→- Enrichment annotations added in step-04: T2
    64→
    65→---
    66→
    67→## Tier Degradation Rules
    68→
    69→### Remote Source at Forge/Deep Tier
    70→
    71→When `source_repo` is a remote URL (GitHub URL or owner/repo format) and the tier is Forge or Deep:
    72→
    73→- **ast-grep requires local files** — it cannot operate on remote URLs
    74→
    75→**Ephemeral clone strategy (preferred):**
    76→
    77→1. Check `git` availability (`git --version`). `git` is effectively guaranteed at Deep tier (via `gh` dependency) but NOT guaranteed at Forge tier.
    78→2. If `git` is available: perform an ephemeral shallow clone to a system temp path (`{system_temp}/skf-ephemeral-{skill-name}-{timestamp}/`).
    79→3. For create-skill: use `--depth 1 --single-branch --filter=blob:none`; apply sparse-checkout if `include_patterns` are specified.
    80→4. For update-skill: use sparse-checkout scoped to the changed files from the change manifest only. No `--branch` flag — uses the remote default branch (must match the branch used during original create-skill run).
    81→5. If clone succeeds: use the local clone path for AST extraction. All results are T1 with `[AST:...]` citations.
    82→6. Cleanup: delete the temp directory after extraction inventory is built and all data is in context. The clone never persists beyond the extraction step.
    83→
    84→**Fallback (clone fails or `git` unavailable):**
    85→
    86→- The extraction step MUST warn the user explicitly before degrading
    87→- **create-skill:** Warning must include actionable guidance — clone locally and update `source_repo` in the brief to the local path
    88→- **update-skill:** Warning must include actionable guidance — clone locally, re-run [CS] Create Skill with the local path to regenerate provenance data, then re-run the update
    89→- Extraction proceeds using Quick tier strategy (source reading via gh_bridge)
    90→- All results labeled T1-low with `[SRC:...]` citations
    91→- The degradation reason is recorded in the evidence report
    92→
    93→Silent degradation is **forbidden**. The user must always know when AST extraction was skipped and why.
    94→
    95→### AST Tool Unavailable at Forge/Deep Tier
    96→
    97→When the tier is Forge or Deep but ast-grep is not functional:
    98→
    99→- The extraction step MUST warn the user explicitly before degrading
   100→- Warning must include actionable guidance: run [SF] Setup Forge to detect tools
   101→- Extraction proceeds using Quick tier strategy
   102→- All results labeled T1-low
   103→- The degradation reason is recorded in the evidence report
   104→
   105→### Per-File AST Failure
   106→
   107→When ast-grep fails on an individual file (parse error, unsupported syntax):
   108→
   109→- Fall back to source reading for **that file only**
   110→- Other files continue with AST extraction
   111→- The affected file's results are labeled T1-low; unaffected files retain T1
   112→- Log a warning noting which file degraded and why
   113→

  "old_string": "---\n\n## Tier Degradation Rules",
  "new_string": "---\n\n## AST Extraction Protocol\n\nWhen AST tools are available (Forge/Deep tier), follow this deterministic protocol to prevent output overflow on large codebases. The file count from step-01's file tree determines the extraction strategy.\n\n### Decision Tree\n\n```\nFiles in scope ≤ 100\n  → Use ast-grep MCP tool: find_code(pattern, max_results=100, output_format=\"text\")\n  → Parse compact text output directly into extraction inventory\n\nFiles in scope ≤ 500\n  → Use ast-grep MCP tool: find_code_by_rule(yaml, max_results=150, output_format=\"text\")\n  → Use scoped YAML rules (see recipes below) to filter at the AST level\n  → Parse compact text output into extraction inventory\n\nFiles in scope > 500\n  → CLI streaming fallback: ast-grep --json=stream + line-by-line Python processing\n  → Process in directory batches, cap per-batch output\n  → Merge batch results into extraction inventory\n```\n\n### Safety Valve\n\nIf any ast-grep operation (MCP or CLI) produces output that exceeds 200 entries, visibly causes a timeout, or returns an error related to output size: immediately switch to the CLI streaming fallback with `--json=stream`. Do not retry the same approach.\n\n### MCP Tool Usage (Preferred)\n\n**Simple pattern search:**\n\n```\nfind_code(\n  project_folder=\"{source_path}\",\n  pattern=\"async def $NAME($$PARAMS)\",\n  language=\"python\",\n  max_results=100,\n  output_format=\"text\"\n)\n```\n\n**Scoped YAML rule search (for larger repos):**\n\n```\nfind_code_by_rule(\n  project_folder=\"{source_path}\",\n  yaml=\"id: public-api\\nlanguage: python\\nrule:\\n  pattern: 'def $NAME($$PARAMS)'\\n  not:\\n    pattern: 'def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'\\n  inside:\\n    kind: module\\n    stopBy: end\",\n  max_results=150,\n  output_format=\"text\"\n)\n```\n\n### CLI Streaming Fallback\n\nWhen MCP tools are unavailable or the repo exceeds 500 files in scope, use `--json=stream` (NEVER `--json` or `--json=pretty`) with line-by-line Python processing:\n\n```bash\nast-grep -p '{pattern}' -l {language} --json=stream {path} | python3 -c \"\nimport sys, json\nfor line in sys.stdin:\n    try:\n        m = json.loads(line)\n        v = m.get('metaVariables',{})\n        name = v.get('single',{}).get('NAME',{}).get('text','')\n        if name and not name.startswith('_'):\n            f = m.get('file','')\n            ln = m.get('range',{}).get('start',{}).get('line',0)+1\n            sig = m.get('text','').split(chr(10))[0].strip()\n            print(f'[AST:{f}:L{ln}] {sig}')\n    except: pass\n\" | head -200\n```\n\n**Critical constraints:**\n\n- ALWAYS use `--json=stream` — never `--json` (loads entire array into memory)\n- ALWAYS process line-by-line (`for line in sys.stdin`) — never `json.load(sys.stdin)`\n- ALWAYS cap output with `| head -N` as a safety valve\n- For repos > 500 files, process in directory batches of 20-50 files each\n\n### YAML Rule Recipes by Language\n\n**Python — public functions:**\n\n```yaml\nid: python-public-functions\nlanguage: python\nrule:\n  pattern: 'def $NAME($$PARAMS)'\n  not:\n    pattern: 'def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'\n  inside:\n    kind: module\n    stopBy: end\n```\n\n**Python — public classes:**\n\n```yaml\nid: python-public-classes\nlanguage: python\nrule:\n  pattern: 'class $NAME($$BASES)'\n  not:\n    pattern: 'class _$NAME($$BASES)'\n  inside:\n    kind: module\n    stopBy: end\n```\n\n**JavaScript/TypeScript — exported functions:**\n\n```yaml\nid: js-exported-functions\nlanguage: typescript\nrule:\n  pattern: 'export function $NAME($$PARAMS)'\n```\n\n**JavaScript/TypeScript — exported constants:**\n\n```yaml\nid: js-exported-constants\nlanguage: typescript\nrule:\n  pattern: 'export const $NAME = $VALUE'\n```\n\n**Rust — public functions:**\n\n```yaml\nid: rust-public-functions\nlanguage: rust\nrule:\n  pattern: 'pub fn $NAME($$PARAMS) -> $RET'\n```\n\n**Go — exported functions (capitalized):**\n\n```yaml\nid: go-exported-functions\nlanguage: go\nrule:\n  pattern: 'func $NAME($$PARAMS) $RET'\n  constraints:\n    NAME:\n      regex: '^[A-Z]'\n```\n\n---\n\n## Tier Degradation Rules"
}

 ---
 
+## AST Extraction Protocol
+
+When AST tools are available (Forge/Deep tier), follow this deterministic protocol to prevent output overflow on large codebases. The file count from step-01's file tree determines the extraction strategy.
+
+### Decision Tree
+
+```
+Files in scope ≤ 100
+  → Use ast-grep MCP tool: find_code(pattern, max_results=100, output_format="text")
+  → Parse compact text output directly into extraction inventory
+
+Files in scope ≤ 500
+  → Use ast-grep MCP tool: find_code_by_rule(yaml, max_results=150, output_format="text")
+  → Use scoped YAML rules (see recipes below) to filter at the AST level
+  → Parse compact text output into extraction inventory
+
+Files in scope > 500
+  → CLI streaming fallback: ast-grep --json=stream + line-by-line Python processing
+  → Process in directory batches, cap per-batch output
+  → Merge batch results into extraction inventory
+```
+
+### Safety Valve
+
+If any ast-grep operation (MCP or CLI) produces output that exceeds 200 entries, visibly causes a timeout, or returns an error related to output size: immediately switch to the CLI streaming fallback with `--json=stream`. Do not retry the same approach.
+
+### MCP Tool Usage (Preferred)
+
+**Simple pattern search:**
+
+```
+find_code(
+  project_folder="{source_path}",
+  pattern="async def $NAME($$PARAMS)",
+  language="python",
+  max_results=100,
+  output_format="text"
+)
+```
+
+**Scoped YAML rule search (for larger repos):**
+
+```
+find_code_by_rule(
+  project_folder="{source_path}",
+  yaml="id: public-api\nlanguage: python\nrule:\n  pattern: 'def $NAME($$PARAMS)'\n  not:\n    pattern: 'def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'\n  inside:\n    kind: module\n    stopBy: end",
+  max_results=150,
+  output_format="text"
+)
+```
+
+### CLI Streaming Fallback
+
+When MCP tools are unavailable or the repo exceeds 500 files in scope, use `--json=stream` (NEVER `--json` or `--json=pretty`) with line-by-line Python processing:
+
+```bash
+ast-grep -p '{pattern}' -l {language} --json=stream {path} | python3 -c "
+import sys, json
+for line in sys.stdin:
+    try:
+        m = json.loads(line)
+        v = m.get('metaVariables',{})
+        name = v.get('single',{}).get('NAME',{}).get('text','')
+        if name and not name.startswith('_'):
+            f = m.get('file','')
+            ln = m.get('range',{}).get('start',{}).get('line',0)+1
+            sig = m.get('text','').split(chr(10))[0].strip()
+            print(f'[AST:{f}:L{ln}] {sig}')
+    except: pass
+" | head -200
+```
+
+**Critical constraints:**
+
+- ALWAYS use `--json=stream` — never `--json` (loads entire array into memory)
+- ALWAYS process line-by-line (`for line in sys.stdin`) — never `json.load(sys.stdin)`
+- ALWAYS cap output with `| head -N` as a safety valve
+- For repos > 500 files, process in directory batches of 20-50 files each
+
+### YAML Rule Recipes by Language
+
+**Python — public functions:**
+
+```yaml
+id: python-public-functions
+language: python
+rule:
+  pattern: 'def $NAME($$PARAMS)'
+  not:
+    pattern: 'def _$NAME($$PARAMS)'
+  inside:
+    kind: module
+    stopBy: end
+```
+
+**Python — public classes:**
+
+```yaml
+id: python-public-classes
+language: python
+rule:
+  pattern: 'class $NAME($$BASES)'
+  not:
+    pattern: 'class _$NAME($$BASES)'
+  inside:
+    kind: module
+    stopBy: end
+```
+
+**JavaScript/TypeScript — exported functions:**
+
+```yaml
+id: js-exported-functions
+language: typescript
+rule:
+  pattern: 'export function $NAME($$PARAMS)'
+```
+
+**JavaScript/TypeScript — exported constants:**
+
+```yaml
+id: js-exported-constants
+language: typescript
+rule:
+  pattern: 'export const $NAME = $VALUE'
+```
+
+**Rust — public functions:**
+
+```yaml
+id: rust-public-functions
+language: rust
+rule:
+  pattern: 'pub fn $NAME($$PARAMS) -> $RET'
+```
+
+**Go — exported functions (capitalized):**
+
+```yaml
+id: go-exported-functions
+language: go
+rule:
+  pattern: 'func $NAME($$PARAMS) $RET'
+  constraints:
+    NAME:
+      regex: '^[A-Z]'
+```
+
+---
+
 ## Tier Degradation Rules
 
 ### Remote Source at Forge/Deep Tier

Never use `ast-grep --json` (without `=stream`) — it loads the entire result set into memory and will fail on large codebases.
+
 1. Detect language from brief or file extensions
-2. Use `ast_bridge.scan_definitions(path, language)` for each file in scope

+   - >500 files: use CLI `--json=stream` with line-by-line streaming Python
 3. For each export: extract function name, full signature, parameter types, return type, line number
 4. Use `ast_bridge.detect_co_imports(path, libraries[])` to find integration points
 5. Build extraction rules YAML data for reproducibility
